From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Ever since that happened, I've just had this reaction.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your daily routine or cause you to avoid certain situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely affects things at work. I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there late. And I take the long way around the loading area now to avoid that back corridor. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and I just feel completely scared the whole time I'm in there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations or relationships that have been affected by your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Socially, not so much, but at work, yeah. It's really just that I get super panicked and feel like I have to get out of the room right that second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overly anxious or panicked in situations other than when you think there might be a bat or something flapping around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, everyday stuff doesn't bother me at all. I don't get anxious dealing with customers on the floor or just going about my life. It's literally just... if I think a bat is in the room. That's the only time I ever get worked up like that.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the thought of a bat that causes your panic, or could it be any other animal or creature?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much just bats. I mean, dogs or spiders or mice don't bother me at all. I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to actually hurt me, but... if I see anything that even looks like one, or just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye, my mind just treats it like I'm in real danger. So yeah, it's specifically bats.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of bats, or is this something that started recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. It's been going on for about two years now. It started... well, it was after this one night when a bat actually got into the back corridor while I was working a receiving shift. Ever since that happened, I've just had this reaction.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your daily routine or cause you to avoid certain situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely affects things at work. I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there late. And I take the long way around the loading area now to avoid that back corridor. When I absolutely have to go check the stairwell, I just... I make myself do it, but I do it as fast as I can. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and I just feel completely scared the whole time I'm in there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations or relationships that have been affected by your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Socially, not so much, but at work, yeah. My relationship with my manager is definitely taking a hit. He's... well, he's started questioning whether I can actually handle the shift lead role. Since I keep trading away my closing duties, I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore. It honestly feels like this whole thing could end up costing me the permanent management position I was working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your personal life, outside of work, where you feel like your fear of bats is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of work, it doesn't really come up. I mean, I live in an apartment building, so I don't exactly have to worry about them getting in the attic or anything like that. It's pretty much just at the store where it bothers me. So, yeah, my personal life is mostly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a place outside of work because you thought there might be bats there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of any time that's happened. Like I said, it's pretty much just an issue at work. I don't really go anywhere else where I'd even expect to see one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a social invitation because you thought there might be bats present?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, my friends don't exactly hang out in caves or abandoned buildings. We usually just grab a beer or watch a game, so there's never really been a reason to turn anything down because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered quitting your job because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: I haven't thought about just up and quitting, no. But... it is causing some real problems for me there. My manager has actually started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. Since I keep having to trade away my closing duties, and I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore, it's getting noticed. Honestly, it feels like this whole thing could cost me the permanent management position I've been working toward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Well, I guess what brings me in is that... the second I think there might be a bat or something flapping around the stockroom at my job, my heart just jumps. I start sweating immediately, and I just feel like I need to get out of there right then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described where you felt like you were going to pass out or have a heart attack?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my heart definitely beats really fast, but... I've never felt like I was actually having a heart attack or that I was going to pass out. It's really just that I get super panicked and feel like I have to get out of the room right that second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overly anxious or panicked in situations other than when you think there might be a bat or something flapping around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, everyday stuff doesn't bother me at all. I don't get anxious dealing with customers on the floor or just going about my life. It's literally just... if I think a bat is in the room. That's the only time I ever get worked up like that.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the thought of a bat that causes your panic, or could it be any other animal or creature?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much just bats. I mean, dogs or spiders or mice don't bother me at all. I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to actually hurt me, but... if I see anything that even looks like one, or just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye, my mind just treats it like I'm in real danger. So yeah, it's specifically bats.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of bats, or is this something that started recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. It's been going on for about two years now. It started... well, it was after this one night when a bat actually got into the back corridor while I was working a receiving shift. It's pretty much just at the store where it bothers me. Well, I guess what brings me in is that... the second I think there might be a bat or something flapping around the stockroom at my job, my heart just jumps. I start sweating immediately, and I just feel like I need to get out of there right then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described where you felt like you were going to pass out or have a heart attack?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my heart definitely beats really fast, but... I've never felt like I was actually having a heart attack or that I was going to pass out. It's really just that I get super panicked and feel like I have to get out of the room right that second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overly anxious or panicked in situations other than when you think there might be a bat or something flapping around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, everyday stuff doesn't bother me at all. I don't get anxious dealing with customers on the floor or just going about my life. It's literally just... if I think a bat is in the room. That's the only time I ever get worked up like that.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the thought of a bat that causes your panic, or could it be any other animal or creature?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much just bats. I mean, dogs or spiders or mice don't bother me at all. I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to actually hurt me, but... if I see anything that even looks like one, or just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye, my mind just treats it like I'm in real danger. So yeah, it's specifically bats.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of bats, or is this something that started recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. It's been going on for about two years now. It started... well, it was after this one night when a bat actually got into the back corridor while I was working a receiving shift. Ever since that happened, I've just had this reaction.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your daily routine or cause you to avoid certain situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely affects things at work. I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there late. And I take the long way around the loading area now to avoid that back corridor. When I absolutely have to go check the stairwell, I just... I make myself do it, but I do it as fast as I can. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and I just feel completely scared the whole time I'm in there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations or relationships that have been affected by your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Socially, not so much, but at work, yeah. My relationship with my manager is definitely taking a hit. He's... well, he's started questioning whether I can actually handle the shift lead role. Since I keep trading away my closing duties, I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore. It honestly feels like this whole thing could end up costing me the permanent management position I was working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your personal life, outside of work, where you feel like your fear of bats is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of work, it doesn't really come up. I mean, I live in an apartment building, so I don't exactly have to worry about them getting in the attic or anything like that. It's pretty much just at the store where it bothers me. So, yeah, my personal life is mostly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a place outside of work because you thought there might be bats there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of any time that's happened. Like I said, it's pretty much just an issue at work. I don't really go anywhere else where I'd even expect to see one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a social invitation because you thought there might be bats present?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, my friends don't exactly hang out in caves or abandoned buildings. We usually just grab a beer or watch a game, so there's never really been a reason to turn anything down because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered quitting your job because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: I haven't thought about just up and quitting, no. But... it is causing some real problems for me there. My manager has actually started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. Since I keep having to trade away my closing duties, and I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore, it's getting noticed. Honestly, it feels like this whole thing could cost me the permanent management position I've been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of bats is holding you back from achieving your goals or enjoying your life in any way beyond the issues you're having at work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of work, my life is pretty normal. I still do all the things I want to do, hang out with my friends, all that. It's really just the job where it's causing an issue. I just want to be able to do my job without this getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific task or area at work because you thought there might be a bat present?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I mentioned before, I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there at night. I also started taking the long way around the loading area outside just to avoid it entirely. And when I absolutely have to check the back stairwell... I mean, I force myself to do it, but I do it really, really fast. I'll hold my phone out the whole time and I just feel completely scared and on edge until I get out of there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of bats is causing you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's really just at work. Outside of that, everything is fine. I don't really think about it or run into any problems with it when I'm at home or out doing other things. It's just the stuff at the store that's getting messed up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, my friends don't exactly hang out in caves or abandoned buildings. We usually just grab a beer or watch a game, so there's never really been a reason to turn anything down because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered quitting your job because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: I haven't thought about just up and quitting, no. But... it is causing some real problems for me there. My manager has actually started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. Since I keep having to trade away my closing duties, and I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore, it's getting noticed. Honestly, it feels like this whole thing could cost me the permanent management position I've been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of bats is holding you back from achieving your goals or enjoying your life in any way beyond the issues you're having at work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of work, my life is pretty normal. I still do all the things I want to do, hang out with my friends, all that. It's really just the job where it's causing an issue. I just want to be able to do my job without this getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific task or area at work because you thought there might be a bat present?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I mentioned before, I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there at night. I also started taking the long way around the loading area outside just to avoid it entirely. And when I absolutely have to check the back stairwell... I mean, I force myself to do it, but I do it really, really fast. I'll hold my phone out the whole time and I just feel completely scared and on edge until I get out of there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of bats is causing you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's really just at work. Outside of that, everything is fine. I don't really think about it or run into any problems with it when I'm at home or out doing other things. It's just the stuff at the store that's getting messed up.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress when you think about bats outside of the work context?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm not at work, I don't really think about them. It's not like I'm sitting at home worrying about bats or anything. It's just when I'm at the store, especially near that loading dock or the stairwell, that it really gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and the specific situations we've already talked about, where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing problems?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, there really isn't.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that... the second I think there might be a bat or something flapping around the stockroom at my job, my heart just jumps. I start sweating immediately, and I just feel like I need to get out of there right then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described where you felt like you were going to pass out or have a heart attack?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my heart definitely beats really fast, but... I've never felt like I was actually having a heart attack or that I was going to pass out. It's really just that I get super panicked and feel like I have to get out of the room right that second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overly anxious or panicked in situations other than when you think there might be a bat or something flapping around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, everyday stuff doesn't bother me at all. I don't get anxious dealing with customers on the floor or just going about my life. It's literally just... if I think a bat is in the room. That's the only time I ever get worked up like that.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the thought of a bat that causes your panic, or could it be any other animal or creature?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much just bats. I mean, dogs or spiders or mice don't bother me at all. I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to actually hurt me, but... if I see anything that even looks like one, or just catch a quick flutter in the back hallway out of the corner of my eye, my mind just treats it like I'm in real danger. So yeah, it's specifically bats.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of bats, or is this something that started recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. It's been going on for about two years now. It started... well, it was after this one night when a bat actually got into the back corridor while I was working a receiving shift. Ever since that happened, I've just had this reaction.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your daily routine or cause you to avoid certain situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely affects things at work. I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there late. And I take the long way around the loading area now to avoid that back corridor. When I absolutely have to go check the stairwell, I just... I make myself do it, but I do it as fast as I can. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and I just feel completely scared the whole time I'm in there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations or relationships that have been affected by your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Socially, not so much, but at work, yeah. My relationship with my manager is definitely taking a hit. He's... well, he's started questioning whether I can actually handle the shift lead role. Since I keep trading away my closing duties, I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore. It honestly feels like this whole thing could end up costing me the permanent management position I was working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your personal life, outside of work, where you feel like your fear of bats is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of work, it doesn't really come up. I mean, I live in an apartment building, so I don't exactly have to worry about them getting in the attic or anything like that. It's pretty much just at the store where it bothers me. So, yeah, my personal life is mostly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going to a place outside of work because you thought there might be bats there?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of any time that's happened. Like I said, it's pretty much just an issue at work. I don't really go anywhere else where I'd even expect to see one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a social invitation because you thought there might be bats present?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, my friends don't exactly hang out in caves or abandoned buildings. We usually just grab a beer or watch a game, so there's never really been a reason to turn anything down because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered quitting your job because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: I haven't thought about just up and quitting, no. But... it is causing some real problems for me there. My manager has actually started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role. Since I keep having to trade away my closing duties, and I'm just not as dependable for those after-hours checks anymore, it's getting noticed. Honestly, it feels like this whole thing could cost me the permanent management position I've been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of bats is holding you back from achieving your goals or enjoying your life in any way beyond the issues you're having at work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of work, my life is pretty normal. I still do all the things I want to do, hang out with my friends, all that. It's really just the job where it's causing an issue. I just want to be able to do my job without this getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific task or area at work because you thought there might be a bat present?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I mentioned before, I've been swapping out my closing shifts so I don't have to be there at night. I also started taking the long way around the loading area outside just to avoid it entirely. And when I absolutely have to check the back stairwell... I mean, I force myself to do it, but I do it really, really fast. I'll hold my phone out the whole time and I just feel completely scared and on edge until I get out of there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of bats is causing you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's really just at work. Outside of that, everything is fine. I don't really think about it or run into any problems with it when I'm at home or out doing other things. It's just the stuff at the store that's getting messed up.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress when you think about bats outside of the work context?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm not at work, I don't really think about them. It's not like I'm sitting at home worrying about bats or anything. I mean, dogs or spiders or mice don't bother me at all. I'll hold my phone out like a flashlight and I just feel completely scared the whole time I'm in there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations or relationships that have been affected by your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Socially, not so much, but at work, yeah. It's pretty much just at the store where it bothers me. Like I said, it's pretty much just an issue at work.
